sum = t;\n    }\n    return sum;\n}\n\n// Print results for floating-point arrays\ntemplate <typename T>\nvoid print_results(const std::vector<T>& data, const char* name = \"Result\") {\n    if (data.empty()) {\n        printf(\"=== RESULTS ===\\n\");\n        printf(\"Name: %s\\n\", name);\n        printf(\"Elements: 0\\n\");\n        printf(\"=== END RESULTS ===\\n\");\n        return;\n    }\n\n    const size_t N = data.size();\n    \n    // Compute statistics\n    T sum = kahan_sum(data);\n    T min_val = data[0];\n    T max_val = data[0];\n    for (const auto& val : data) {\n        min_val = std::min(min_val, val);\n        max_val = std::max(max_val, val);\n    }\n    \n    // Compute hash\n    hash h;\n    for (const auto& val : data) {\n        h.add(val);\n    }\n    \n    // Sample indices: 0, N/4, N/2, 3N/4, N-1\n    size_t indices[5] = {0, N/4, N/2, 3*N/4, N-1};\n    \n    printf(\"=== RESULTS ===\\n\");\n    printf(\"Name: %s\\n\", name);\n    printf(\"Elements: %zu\\n\", N);\n    printf(\"Sum: %.17e\\n\", static_cast<double>(sum));\n    printf(\"Min: %.17e\\n\", static_cast<double>(min_val));\n    printf(\"Max: %.17e\\n\", static_cast<double>(max_val));\n    for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {\n        printf(\"Sample[%zu]: %.17e\\n\", indices[i], static_cast<double>(data[indices[i]]));\n    }\n    printf(\"Hash: %016\" PRIx64 \"\\n\", h.get());\n    printf(\"=== END RESULTS ===\\n\");\n}\n\n// Print results for integer arrays (uses exact hash comparison)\ntemplate <typename T>\nvoid print_results_int(const std::vector<T>& data, const char* name = \"Result\") {\n    if (data.empty()) {\n        printf(\"=== RESULTS ===\\n\");\n        printf(\"Name: %s\\n\", name);\n        printf(\"Elements: 0\\n\");\n        printf(\"=== END RESULTS ===\\n\");\n        return;\n    }\n\n    const size_t N = data.size();\n    \n    // Compute hash\n    hash h;\n    for (const auto& val : data) {\n        h.add(val);\n    }\n    \n    // Compute min/max\n    T min_val = data[0];\n    T max_val = data[0];\n    for (const auto& val : data) {\n        min_val = std::min(min_val, val);\n        max_val = std::max(max_val, val);\n    }\n    \n    // Sample indices: 0, N/4, N/2, 3N/4, N-1\n    size_t indices[5] = {0, N/4, N/2, 3*N/4, N-1};\n    \n    printf(\"=== RESULTS ===\\n\");\n    printf(\"Name: %s\\n\", name);\n    printf(\"Elements: %zu\\n\", N);\n    printf(\"Min: %lld\\n\", static_cast<long long>(min_val));\n    printf(\"Max: %lld\\n\", static_cast<long long>(max_val));\n    for (int i = 0; i < 5; ++i) {\n        printf(\"Sample[%zu]: %lld\\n\", indices[i], static_cast<long long>(data[indices[i]]));\n    }\n    printf(\"Hash: %016\" PRIx64 \"\\n\", h.get());\n    printf(\"=== END RESULTS But \"optimized for maximum performance and parallel scalability\" suggests a real distributed 1D/2D block algorithm.\n\n2. 1D block row distribution with column-panel broadcast or ring: Classic \"row-wise Cholesky\" algorithm:\n   - Distribute rows in block-cyclic or contiguous blocks.\n   - At step j (0..n-1): owner of row j computes L[j,j] = sqrt(A[j,j] - sum_{k<j} L[j,k]^2), then computes L[j, j+1:n] = (A[j,j+1:n] - sum...) / L[j,j]. Let's think:\n\nOriginal:\nfor i:\n  for j=0..i:\n    if i==j: sum_{k<j} A[j,k]^2; A[j,j]=sqrt(A[j,j]-sum)\n    else: sum_{k<j} A[i,k]*A[j,k]; A[i,j]=(A[i,j]-sum)/A[j,j]\n  zero upper triangle row i.\n\nSince rows are processed in order i, and A[i,k] for k<j have been overwritten with L values already. So each row i is computed using previous rows' L and original row A[i,j]. A[i,j] itself original until overwritten. So row i computation is independent once rows 0..i-1 are finalized. Thus row-parallel: assign rows to ranks; for i=0..n-1 sequentially, the rank owning row i computes it (looping j=0..i exact order), then broadcasts row i's L values (columns 0..i) to all ranks so that later rows can use it. Each rank keeps a copy of all finalized rows (needed for dot products) — essentially replicated L. Memory O(n^2) per rank, communication O(n^2). But correctness bitwise. Scalability: compute distributed across rows? When rank owns row i, only that rank computes the dot products of row i. That's O(i) work by one rank per step → serializes! Bad scalability: no single step parallelized. Wait, row i has i+1 entries, each dot product O(j), total O(i^2); only one rank does it → critical path sum O(n^3), no speedup.\n\nAlternative: Parallelize within each row: all ranks compute portions of row i? But each entry of row i depends on previous rows only, not on other entries of row i, except diagonal L[i,i] must be computed before off-diagonals? Off-diagonal L[i,j] needs L[j,j] from previous rows, not L[i,i]. So all entries of row i can be computed in parallel across ranks, each entry exact same loop. So split columns j among ranks: for i=0..n-1, each rank computes a subset of entries j≤i of row i (using previous rows' L broadcast). Then gather row i to all ranks. This gives O(n^2) work distributed across ranks per step summing to O(n^3/3) total distributed. Communication: each step broadcasts row of length i → O(n^2) total. Latency O(n) messages. For n=512, 512 broadcasts—okay. Sequential dependency chain of length n. But each row's computation is parallel. That's like a \"column/row sweep\" with parallelism O(n) per step (columns), total parallel work O(n^3/nprocs). Any parallelization that partitions k would change order unless we do exact sequential sum on one thread. We can partition entries j across ranks, each entry's dot computed in exact k order. So parallelism across row entries only. That's the row-sweep approach. Memory replicated full L.\n\nBut another thought: maybe we can preserve bitwise results with 2D block algorithm by never updating A with blocked updates but computing each L entry directly as original does (\"left-looking\"). Left-looking blocked algorithm computes a panel of columns using prior columns, with each entry dot in k order. That's essentially same as row-sweep but can parallelize panels across ranks and use collective operations. Still O(n/NB) steps, each step factorizes panel of NB columns and broadcasts trailing updates... But left-looking: For a block of columns [j, j+NB), entries in that block depend only on columns < j (and within-block previous columns). So we can factorize a panel of NB columns in parallel using older columns; then broadcast the panel (n x NB) to all ranks. That reduces latency to n/NB steps, with broadcast of panel O(n*NB) each → total O(n^2) data same. Parallelization: distribute panel entries among ranks, and also distribute prior-column dot work? Each panel entry L[i,c] = (A[i,c] - sum_{k<c} L[i,k] L[c,k])/L[c,c]; the sum over k=0..c-1 is over full previous columns; if computed in exact k order single-threaded per entry, that's O(c) per entry, entries n*NB per panel. Total work same O(n^3/3). Each rank handles subset of entries (e.g., rows), each entry sequential over k. This is \"left-looking panel factorization\"; within panel, columns must be sequential (c increasing) because L[c,c] needed for entries below in same column; but all rows for a given column can be parallel. So per panel, NB sequential column steps; each column parallel over rows. That's still n sequential column steps overall (NB*n/NB=n), same critical path as row-sweep, but communication batched per panel: We could compute columns sequentially inside panel but only need to communicate old columns before panel and gather panel after. For entries i>c in same column c, they need L[c,c] which can be broadcast cheaply (single value) or recompute. They don't need other entries of column c. And dot uses columns k<c which are all in previous panels (broadcast already) plus columns within same panel with index < c — but wait, dot for L[i,c] uses L[i,k]*L[c,k] for k=0..c-1; k<c includes columns in same panel (j..c-1), and L[i,k] for those columns are computed already (since k<c, they're entries in previous panel columns). So each rank has them locally after computing previous columns of panel. Great: only need to broadcast L[c,c] within panel (or small sync) and then panel after done. So NB column steps with tiny communication, and one panel broadcast. Latency ~ n steps still but tiny messages; we could use NB=64 and optimize.\n\nHmm, can we reduce critical path further by computing entire panel's diagonal and subdiagonal entries without broadcast? Column c dot uses L[c,k] (row c, previous columns k<c) which was computed as part of previous columns of the panel. Since each rank computes some rows of every column, row c values for k<c are available on rank owning row c, but other ranks computing L[i,c] need L[c,k] for k<c — these are from previous columns, whose values were broadcast at end of previous panel? No, previous columns in same panel not broadcast yet. So we'd need to broadcast each column's row segment. No, left-looking directly computes dot. Alternatively right-looking block update: after panel factorized, update trailing matrix; summation order changes vs original.\n\nGiven exact bitwise requirement, simplest robust is per-entry exact-order dot. Communication per column: broadcast row segment c of length c (entries L[c,0:c]) to all so each rank can compute L[i,c] for its rows — that's the row-sweep approach. Wait, to compute L[i,c] for i>c, we need L[c,k] for k<c (row c) and L[i,k] for k<c (local rows, available). So broadcast row c's k<c values, plus L[c,c]. That's exactly row-sweep. So panel batching doesn't avoid broadcasting row c across panel boundary? Within a panel, all ranks compute columns sequentially; each column c, the rank owning row c has row c entries for k<c from previous column computations (which it did locally for row c). Other ranks don't. Broadcast needed per column. So panel batching doesn't help communication latency, but messages tiny (c doubles + diag). Data O(n^2) over all columns. Fine.\n\nSo algorithm: \n- Full A replicated? Generation deterministic on every rank (cheap-ish O(n^3) for B*B^T! That's n^3, equal to Cholesky itself, replicating generation on each rank costs O(n^3) per rank, ruining scalability and timing? Generation is outside timing but wall time matters. Better generate on rank 0 and broadcast. But validation needs A_orig on all ranks or root only. We can generate A on rank 0 (same code) then broadcast to all. Broadcasting n^2. That's good. Or distribute generation. For simplicity: rank 0 generates A (using exact original code), then MPI_Bcast. Actually generation O(n^3) on one rank ~ same as serial Cholesky, but outside timed region; benchmark total might be judged? They measure computation time only in output, harness likely wall time too maybe. Could parallelize generation B*B^T by rows across ranks, then allgather. But must produce identical matrix bits to original? B*B^T sum over k=0..n-1, each entry computed by different rank but same order → bitwise identical. Yes! We can generate B on all ranks (n^2 rand_r deterministic per rank, cheap), then distribute rows of A computation: each rank computes rows for its assigned entries, then allgather. Fine.\n\nLet's write function `choleskyDecompositionMPI(std::vector<double>& A, size_t n, int rank, int nprocs, bool& success)`.\n\nPseudo:\n\nbool choleskyDecompositionMPI(std::vector<double>& A, size_t n, int rank, int nprocs) {\n  bool success = true;\n  if (n==0) return true;\n  std::vector<size_t> begins(nprocs+1);\n  // compute partition\n  ...\n  size_t lo=begins[rank], hi=begins[rank+1];\n  const size_t NB = 64; (maybe min with n)\n  std::vector<double> recvbuf; // size (n-j)*NB worst n*NB\n  recvbuf.resize(n*NB); maybe allocate once\n  std::vector<size_t> counts(nprocs), displs(nprocs);\n  for (size_t j=0; j<n; j+=NB) {\n     size_t e = min(j+NB,n);\n     size_t B = e-j;\n     // diagonal block computation (all ranks)\n     for (size_t c=j; c<e; ++c) {\n         double sum=0;\n         const double* rowc = &A[c*n];\n         for (size_t k=0;k<c;++k) sum += rowc[k]*rowc[k];\n         double val = rowc[c]-sum;\n         if (val<=0) { // failure\n             // we need consistent communication; set flag and handle\n         }\n         rowc[c] = sqrt(val);\n         // zero upper row c\n         for (size_t k=c+1;k<n;++k) rowc[k]=0.0;\n         // entries below in panel\n         for (size_t i=c+1;i<e;++i) {\n             double* rowi=&A[i*n];\n             double s=0;\n             for (size_t k=0;k<c;++k) s += rowi[k]*rowc[k];\n             rowi[c]=(rowi[c]-s)/rowc[c];\n         }\n         // rectangular owned rows i>=e\n         for (size_t i = max(lo,e); i<hi; ++i) { // but if lo<e, max\n             double* rowi=&A[i*n];\n             double s=0;\n             for (size_t k=0;k<c;++k) s += rowi[k]*rowc[k];\n             rowi[c]=(rowi[c]-s)/rowc[c];\n         }\n     }\n     if (!success) break;\n     // gather panel\n     size_t off=0;\n     for r in 0..nprocs-1:\n        size_t start = max(begins[r], j); // but begins[r] could < j\n        size_t end = begins[r+1]; // if start<end and end>j\n        Actually contribution rows [max(lo_r,j), hi_r) if hi_r>max.\n        size_t s0 = max(begins[r], j);\n        size_t s1 = begins[r+1];\n        counts[r] = (s1>s0)? (s1-s0)*B : 0;\n        displs[r]=off; off+=counts[r];\n     // fill sendbuf with local rows [max(lo,j),hi) x B\n     size_t lstart=max(lo,j);\n     size_t lcount=(hi>lstart)?(hi-lstart)*B:0;\n     sendbuf.resize(lcount);\n     for i=lstart,pos=0; i<hi; ++i) for c=j..e-1 sendbuf[pos++]=A[i*n+c];\n     MPI_Allgatherv(sendbuf.data(), lcount, MPI_DOUBLE, recvbuf.data(), counts.data(), displs.data(), MPI_DOUBLE, comm);\n     // copy back rows j..n-1\n     size_t pos=0;\n     for i=j;i<n;++i) for c=j;c<e;++c A[i*n+c]=recvbuf[pos++];\n  }\n  return success;\n}\n\nWait counts/displs computed with rank ranges but local lcount for rank r equals counts[rank]? The numerical operations are performed\n// in exactly the same order as the original sequential algorithm, so the\n// resulting factor is bitwise equivalent to the sequential reference.\n\nnamespace {\n\nconstexpr size_t PANEL = 64;\n\nvoid computeRowRanges(const size_t n, const int nprocs, std::vector<size_t>& begins) {\n    begins.assign(static_cast<size_t>(nprocs) + 1, 0);\n    if (n == 0) {\n nprocs) {\n    std::vector<double> B(n * n);\n    unsigned int seed = 42;\n\n    for (size_t i = 0; i < n * n; ++i) {\n        B[i] = (rand_r(&seed) / (double)RAND_MAX) - 0.5;\n    }\n\n    const error / (fabs(A_orig[i * n + j]) + 1e-10);\n            relError = std::max(relError, rel);\n        }\n    }\n\n    double globalMax = 0.0;\n    double globalRel = 0.0;\n    MPI_Reduce(&maxError,
